For the other direction, suppose that
g\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muP\longrightarrow A\in\mathtt{I}
.
Using the above characterisation of
\mathbb{SR}(\mathtt{I})
,
we have that

\hskip 2.04861ptg\hskip 2.04861pt
=
\hskip 2.04861ptg\hskip 2.04861pt

Since
\mathrm{ev}_{{P}}
is an epimorphism
and

g\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{{P}}\in\mathtt{I}
, as
\mathtt{I}
is a subpresheaf of
\mathcal{C}({-},A)
,
we have that
g\in\mathbb{SR}(\mathtt{I})
,
hence
\mathtt{I}\subseteq\mathbb{SR}(\mathtt{I})
.
∎

We should additionally observe that the ideal correspondence we establish is a lattice isomorphism. Given representable ideals
\mathtt{I,J}
in
\mathcal{C}({-},A)
, we write
\mathtt{I}\subseteq\mathtt{J}
if
\mathtt{I}(P)\subseteq\mathtt{J}(P)
for all
P\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
. By
4.1
, this is equivalent to
\mathtt{I}(V)\subseteq\mathtt{J}(V)
for all
V\in\mathcal{C}
, which, since limits in presheaf categories are pointwise, is equivalent to
\mathtt{I}
being a subobject of
\mathtt{J}
in
[\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}},\mathbf{Vec}]
. Finally, since the Yoneda embedding
よ
is fully faithful, this is equivalent to
I
being a subobject of
J
, where
I,J
are the objects of
\mathcal{C}
representing
\mathtt{I,J}
.

The following is an immediate consequence of
Equation 5.2
and
Equation 5.1
:

Lemma 5.6
.

Given representable ideals
\mathtt{I,J}
in
\mathcal{C}({-},A)
such that
\mathtt{I}\subseteq\mathtt{J}
, we have
\mathbb{R}(\mathtt{I})\subseteq\mathbb{R}(\mathtt{J})
. Similarly, given
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideals
\mathfrak{I,J}
in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
such that
\mathfrak{I}\subseteq\mathfrak{J}
, we have
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I})\subseteq\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{J})
.

Since there is a lattice isomorphism between ideals in
A
and representable ideals in
\mathcal{C}({-},A)
, we find the following:

Theorem 5.7
.

The maps
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{S}
induce mutually inverse lattice isomorphisms between the lattice of ideals in
A
and the lattice of
\,\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideals in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
.

6.
Nilpotent ideals

6.1.
Products of ideals

Definition 6.0
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an abelian monoidal category whose tensor product is right exact in both variables. Let
(B,\mu_{B},\eta_{B})
be an algebra object in
\mathcal{D}
and let
I,J
be ideals in
B
, with respective inclusion morphisms
\iota_{I},\iota_{J}
into
B
. We define the product ideal
IJ
by

IJ=\operatorname{Im}(I\otimes J\xrightarrow{\iota_{I}\otimes\iota_{J}}B\otimes B\xrightarrow{\mu_{B}}B).

It is not difficult to verify that
IJ
indeed defines an ideal.

In the case
\mathcal{D}=[\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}},\mathbf{Vec}]
, for ideals
\EuRoman{I,J}
in
\mathcal{C}({-},A)
, the ideal
\EuRoman{IJ}
is given as the image of the map

\EuRoman{I}\oast\EuRoman{J}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}({-},A)\oast\mathcal{C}({-},A)\cong\mathcal{C}({-},A\otimes A)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{C}({-},\mu)}\mathcal{C}({-},A).

As such, following the description of Day convolution in
Equation 2.3
, we find that

\EuRoman{IJ}(U)=\left\{\mu\circ(g\otimes h)\circ\varphi\mid g\in\EuRoman{I}(V),f\in\EuRoman{J}(V^{\prime})\text{ and }\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(U,V\otimes V^{\prime})\right\}.

However, there is a technical subtlety here. Let
I,J
be ideals in
A
.
Then in general the presheaves
\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(I)\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(J)
and
\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(IJ)
need not be the same. Indeed, since
よ
does generally not preserve epimorphisms, we find the following:

\displaystyle\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(I)\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(J)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}({-},I)\mathcal{C}({-},J)=\operatorname{Im}(\mu_{\mathcal{C}({-},A)}\circ(\iota_{\mathcal{C}({-},I)}\oast\iota_{\mathcal{C}({-},J)}))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Coker}\operatorname{Ker}(\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(\mu_{A}\circ(\iota_{I}\otimes\iota_{J})))\cong\operatorname{Coker}(\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(\operatorname{Ker}((\mu_{A}\circ(\iota_{I}\otimes\iota_{J})))))

\displaystyle\not\cong\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(\operatorname{Coker}\operatorname{Ker}(\mu_{A}\circ(\iota_{I}\otimes\iota_{J})))\cong\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(\operatorname{Im}(\mu_{A}\circ(\iota_{I}\otimes\iota_{J})))=\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(IJ).

The first isomorphism above uses the identification
\operatorname{Im}(f)=\operatorname{Coker}(\operatorname{Ker}(f))=\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{Coker}(f))
which can be made in an abelian category.
However, the two presheaves agree on projectives:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
I,J
be ideals in
A
. Then
{\mathcal{C}({-},IJ)}_{|\mathcal{C}_{p}}
is given by

\mathcal{C}(P,IJ)=\left\{\mu\circ(g\otimes h)\circ\varphi\mid g\in\mathcal{C}(Q,I),f\in\mathcal{C}(Q^{\prime},J)\text{ and }\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q\otimes Q^{\prime})\right\}.

Proof.

By
[
St2
, Theorem 4.27]
, the composite functor
\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}[\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}},\mathbf{Vec}]\xrightarrow{{({-})}_{|\mathcal{C}_{p}}}[\mathcal{C}_{p}^{\operatorname{op}},\mathbf{vec}]
is a monoidal equivalence, and so

{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(IJ)}_{|\mathcal{C}_{p}}\cong{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(I)}_{|\mathcal{C}_{p}}{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}(J)}_{|\mathcal{C}_{p}}
, and the latter is obtained by Day convolution, analogously to
Equation 2.3
.
∎

Continuing the correspondence between ideals in
A
and mixed subfunctors of
\mathcal{C}({-},A)
, we find:

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathtt{I,J}
be mixed subfunctors of
\mathcal{C}({-},A)
. We define the mixed subfunctor
\mathtt{IJ}
by

\mathtt{IJ}(P)=\left\{\mu\circ(g\otimes h)\circ\varphi\mid g\in\mathtt{I}(Q),f\in\mathtt{J}(Q^{\prime})\text{ and }\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q\otimes Q^{\prime})\right\}

The following is the special case of
Subsection 6.1
, where
\mathcal{D}=\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{A,A})
is the category of pro-functors and
A=\mathcal{A}({-},{-})
:

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{I,J}
be a pair of ideals in a
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{A}
. We define the ideal
\mathcal{IJ}
by

\mathcal{IJ}(X,Y)=\operatorname{Span}(\left\{g\circ f\mid f\in\mathcal{J}(X,X^{\prime}),g\in\mathcal{I}(X^{\prime},Y)\text{ and }X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}),

for
X,Y\in\mathcal{A}
.

While we give a proof of
6.2
below, we remark that it also is a special case of
Subsection 6.1
, specialised to the monoidal category of
Tambara modules on
\mathcal{C}
(see
[
St1
, Proposition 10.16]
):

Lemma 6.2
.

If
\mathcal{I,J}
are ideals in a module category
\mathcal{M}
over a monoidal category
\mathcal{D}
, and both
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
are
\mathcal{D}
-stable, then
\mathcal{IJ}
is
\mathcal{D}
-stable.

Proof.

Let
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\circ f_{i}\in\mathcal{IJ}
, where

f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{J},g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\in\mathcal{I}
and
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in\Bbbk
. Further, let
V\in\mathcal{D}
.
Then
V\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\circ f_{i}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}(V\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g_{i})\circ(V\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f_{i})\in\mathcal{IJ}
, since
V\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
and
V\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f_{i}\in\mathcal{J}
by
\mathcal{D}
-stability of
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
respectively.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{I,J}
be a pair of
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideals in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
. Then
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I})\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{J})=\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{IJ})
.

Proof.

We first show the “
\subseteq
” direction. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{p}(P,Q\otimes Q^{\prime}),g\in\mathfrak{I}(Q,Q^{\prime\prime})
and let
f\in\mathfrak{J}(Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime\prime})
, with epimorphisms
Q^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{q}\mathbb{1}
and
Q^{\prime\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{q^{\prime}}\mathbb{1}
.
Let
\mathbb{S}(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(q\otimes A)\circ g\circ(Q\otimes\eta)
and similarly let
\mathbb{S}(f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(q^{\prime}\otimes A)\circ f\circ(Q^{\prime}\otimes A)
.
Following
Subsection 6.1
, a general element of
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I})\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{J})
is of the form
\mu\circ\mathbb{S}(g)\otimes\mathbb{S}(f)\circ\varphi
.
The “
\subseteq
” direction follows by

\hskip 5.0ptg\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptf\hskip 5.0pt
=

\hskip 5.0ptg\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptf\hskip 5.0pt
=

\hskip 5.0ptg\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptf\hskip 5.0pt

where the unlabelled solid box represents the morphism
\varphi
, the morphism enclosed by the upper dotted box
lies in
\mathfrak{I}
, and the morphism enclosed by the lower dotted box lies in
\mathfrak{J}
since
\mathfrak{J}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideal. Thus, the boxes indicate how to realise the right-hand side as a composite of a morphism in
\mathfrak{I}
with a morphism in
\mathfrak{J}
, and hence an element of
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{IJ})
. The first equality holds since
g
is a right
A
-module morphism.

We now show the “
\supseteq
” direction. If we assume
Q=Q^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and remove the assumption that there is an epimorphism
q^{\prime}\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muQ^{\prime\prime\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{1}
, we can write a general element of
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{IJ})
as

\mathbb{S}(g\circ f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(q\otimes A)\circ(g\circ f)\circ(Q^{\prime}\otimes\eta).

The “
\supseteq
” direction follows by

\hskip 5.0ptf\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptg\hskip 5.0pt
=
\hskip 5.0ptf\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptg\hskip 5.0pt
=
\hskip 5.0ptf\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptg\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptf\hskip 5.0pt
\hskip 5.0ptg\hskip 5.0pt
=

where the upper morphisms enclosed by dotted boxes lie in
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I})
and
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{J})
, and the morphism in the lower dotted box plays the role of
\varphi
in the description of
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I})\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{J})
. Thus, left-hand side above presents a general element of
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{IJ})
, and right-hand side illustrates how to realise it as an element of
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I})\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{J})
, proving the result.
∎

Definition 6.3
.

An ideal
I
in
A
is said to be
nilpotent
if there is
k
such that
I^{k}=0
.

Similarly, a
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideal
\mathfrak{J}
in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
is said to be nilpotent if
\mathfrak{J}^{k}=0
for some
k
.

Corollary 6.4
.

An ideal
I
of
A
is nilpotent if and only if so is the
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideal
\mathfrak{I}
in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
corresponding to
I
under the isomorphism of
Theorem 5.7
.

Proof.

This follows directly from
\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I}^{k})={\mathbb{S}(\mathfrak{I})}^{k}
by
6.3
.
∎

6.2.
The
\mathcal{C}
-module radical and exact algebras

Recall that for an additive
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{A}
, its
Jacobson radical

\operatorname{Rad}_{\mathcal{A}}
(see e.g.
[
ASS
, Appendix A.3]
,
[
Kr
, Section 2]
) is the unique two-sided ideal
\mathcal{J}
in
\mathcal{A}
such that for any
X\in\mathcal{A}
, the ideal
\mathcal{J}(X,X)\subseteq\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
is the Jacobson radical. In the case when
\mathcal{A}
is
\operatorname{Hom}
-finite, it is the greatest ideal in
\mathcal{A}
which does not contain any non-zero idempotents.

Definition 6.4
.

We define the
\mathcal{C}
-module radical
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
by setting, for
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
:

(6.1)

\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}(P,P^{\prime})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left\{f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A,P^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A)\ \Bigg|\ \begin{aligned} &Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f\in\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}}(Q\otimes P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A,Q\otimes P^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A)\\
&\text{for all }Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}\end{aligned}\right\}.

We denote the ideal in
A
representing
\mathbb{S}(\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}})
by
\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, and refer to it as the
\mathcal{C}
-module radical of
A
.

Lemma 6.5
.

The
\mathcal{C}
-module radical is a
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideal in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
.

Proof.

If
g\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
and
f,h
are morphisms of
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
such that
h\circ g\circ f
is defined, then the equality
P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}(g\circ f)=(P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g)\circ(P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f)
implies that
g\circ f\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
and similarly for
h\circ g
.
Thus
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
is an ideal in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
. To see that it is
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable, note that for a fixed
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
, the equality
P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}(Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f)=(P\otimes Q)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f
implies that
P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}(Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f)\in\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}}
for all
P
, so
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

The
\mathcal{C}
-module radical is contained in the
\Bbbk
-linear radical. In other words, for all
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
, we have
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}(P,P^{\prime})\subseteq\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}}(P,P^{\prime})
.

Proof.

It suffices to observe that for
f\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}(P,P^{\prime})
, we have

f=(\mathrm{ev}_{{P}^{\vee}}\otimes P^{\prime}\otimes A)\circ(P\otimes{P}^{\vee}\otimes f)\circ P\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{{P}^{\vee}}\otimes A,

and by definition of
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
we have
P\otimes{P}^{\vee}\otimes f\in\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}}
, and so the right-hand side lies in
\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}}
and thus so does
f
.
∎

Proposition 6.7
.

The
\mathcal{C}
-module radical
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
is the greatest nilpotent
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideal in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
.
In particular,
\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is the greatest nilpotent ideal object in
A
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathfrak{J}
is a nilpotent
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable ideal in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
. Since
\mathfrak{J}
is nilpotent, we have an inclusion
\mathfrak{J}\subseteq\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}}
of ideals in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
.
Then for any
f\in\mathfrak{J}
and any
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
, since
\mathfrak{J}
is
\mathcal{C}_{p}
-stable, we must have
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f\in\mathfrak{J}
and hence also
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f\in\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}}
. Thus
f\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
, and since
f
was arbitrary, we also have
\mathfrak{J}\subseteq\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

Recall that we denote by
{\left(\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}\right)}^{\mathsf{c}}
the unique extension of
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
to an ideal of
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, following
2.2
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and let
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
. Let
R_{1}\xrightarrow{r_{1}}R_{0}\xrightarrow{r_{0}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X
be exact, where
r_{0}
is a projective cover of
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X
.
Then
r_{1}\in{\left(\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}\right)}^{\mathsf{c}}
.

Proof.

We want to first apply
[
St2
, Lemma 4.16]
. Using the notation of
[
St2
]
, we set
\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{C}_{p}
and
\mathbf{M}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
. In order to apply the result, we first observe that the assumption about transitivity of
\mathbf{M}
made therein is used only to conclude that
\left\{P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X\mid P\in\mathcal{S}\right\}\neq\left\{0\right\}
for
X\in\mathbf{M}
non-zero. This latter claim is clearly true in our case, so the claim of
[
St2
, Lemma 4.16]
applies and we may use
[
St2
, Lemma 4.11]
to conclude that for any
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
, the morphism
Q^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}r_{0}

is a projective cover of
Q^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}(Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X)
.
In particular, by
[
St2
, Lemma 4.13]
,
we have that
Q^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}r_{1}\in\operatorname{Rad}_{\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)}

for all
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
, establishing the result.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

In our case,
[
St2
, Lemma 4.16]
produces an idempotent
\mathbf{e}
in the semiring
\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{[\mathcal{C}_{p}]}_{\oplus}
, where
{[\mathcal{C}_{p}]}_{\oplus}
denotes the split Grothendieck semiring.
The space
\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{[\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)]}_{\oplus}
becomes a semiring module with a
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-basis given by
\operatorname{Indec}(\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A))
, the indecomposable objects of
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
.
For
[
St2
, Lemma 4.11]
, it is crucial that for a non-zero non-negative linear combination
v
in
\operatorname{Indec}(\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A))
, the element
\mathbf{e}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}v
again is non-zero. However, since
\mathcal{C}_{p}
has finitely many indecomposables,
\mathbf{e}
by construction is a positive linear combination in
\operatorname{Indec}(\mathcal{C}_{p})
, hence the condition is satisfied if and only if
\left\{P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R\mid P\in\mathcal{C}_{p}\right\}\neq\left\{0\right\}
for all non-zero
R\in\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
.

Remark 6.8
.

By replacing
[\mathcal{C}_{p}^{\operatorname{op}},\mathbf{vecfd}]
with its full subcategory of presheaves which vanish on all but finitely many isomorphism classes of indecomposable projectives, our approach is also applicable to tensor categories which are not finite but have enough projectives such as those coming from Lie superalgebras and infinite fusion categories, with one exception:
6.8
requires finiteness.
Moreover, while the use of the Perron–Frobenius theorem in obtaining the idempotent
\mathbf{e}
of
Subsection 6.2
could perhaps in some cases be replaced by suitable generalisations to infinite-dimensional spaces, it is easy to find tensor categories whose Grothendieck semirings of projectives do not admit idempotents.

For example, for
e\in{[\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\text{-}\mathrm{mod}]}_{\oplus}\cong\mathbb{Z}\{\,V_{i}\mid i=1,2,\ldots\,\}
, letting

m(e)=\max\,\{\,n\mid\text{coefficient of }V_{n}\text{ in }e\text{ is non-zero}\,\},

we find
m(e^{2})=2m(e)-1
, so for
e
idempotent we must have
m=1
, showing that the only idempotent is the unit element.

Theorem 6.9
.

The algebra
A
is exact if and only if
\,\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)=0
.

Proof.

If
\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)=0
, then also
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}=0
and hence also
{\left(\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}\right)}^{\mathsf{c}}=0
. By
6.8
, for
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
and
X\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, the projective cover of
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X
must then be zero, and so
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X
must be projective, which establishes the exactness of
A
.

Conversely, if
A
is exact, then for a morphism
f\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muR\longrightarrow R^{\prime}
of
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
P\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
, we find that
P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}\operatorname{Coker}(f)
is projective, and hence
P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}\operatorname{Coker}(f)
is split.
Thus,
P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}f
is either zero, or not radical. Since the former implies
f=0
, and the latter implies
f\not\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}
, we find
\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}=0
.
∎

7.
Main result

We are now able to state our main result, answering
[
EO2
, Conjecture B.6]
affirmatively.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
A
be an algebra object in a finite tensor category
\mathcal{C}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

A
is exact.

(ii)

\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)=0
.

(iii)

A
has no non-zero nilpotent ideals.

(iv)

A
is a finite direct product of simple algebras.

Proof.

The equivalence of
(i)
and
(ii)
is given in
Theorem 6.9
. The equivalence of
(ii)
and
(iii)
follows immediately from
6.7
. That
(i)
implies
(iv)
is well known, however, for completeness, we include a self-contained proof below in
7.4
.
To conclude the proof, we will show that
(iv)
implies
(iii)
.

Let
A=A_{1}\times A_{2}\times\cdots\times A_{n}
be a finite direct product of simple algebras. The above direct product decomposition is also a decomposition of
A
-
A
-bimodules, and so ideals of
A
are of the form
I=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n}
, where
I_{k}
is an ideal in
A_{k}
. Further, it is easy to verify that

(I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n})(J_{1}\times\cdots\times J_{n})=I_{1}J_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n}J_{n}.

Thus,
I
is nilpotent if and only if
I_{k}
is nilpotent, for
k=1,\ldots,n
. But, by the simplicity assumption, for all
k
, the only nilpotent ideal in
A_{k}
is
0
. Thus the only nilpotent ideal in
A
is
0\times\cdots\times 0=0
.
∎

The rest of this section consists of a self-contained proof of the implication
(i)

\implies

(iv)
in
Theorem 7.1
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
A
be an exact algebra object. Let
\mathcal{J}
be an ideal in
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, let
R,R^{\prime}\in\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
be indecomposable. If
\mathcal{J}(R,R^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{id}_{R},\operatorname{id}_{R^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

Assume
\mathcal{J}(R,R^{\prime})\neq 0
, and let
f\in\mathcal{J}(R,R^{\prime})
be non-zero.
Let
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
be such that there is a split monomorphism
\iota\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muR\hookrightarrow Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A
. Let
\pi
be a retraction for
\iota
. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Q\otimes{\vphantom{\!Q}}^{\vee}\!Q\otimes Q)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q\otimes{\vphantom{\!Q}}^{\vee}\!Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{coev}_{{Q}}\otimes Q)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{coev}_{{Q}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q\otimes{\vphantom{\!Q}}^{\vee}\!Q)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}(f\circ\pi)}

Since
\mathrm{coev}_{{Q}}\otimes Q
is a split mono,
and the bottom arrow is also split by
Q
being projective and
A
being exact,
the outer path of the above diagram defines a split morphism.
On the other hand, since
\mathrm{coev}_{{Q}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R^{\prime}
is monic, and
f
is non-zero, the composite
(\mathrm{coev}_{{Q}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R^{\prime})\circ f
is a non-zero split morphism with an indecomposable domain, and thus a split monomorphism. Since
f\in\mathcal{J}
, we obtain
(\mathrm{coev}_{{Q}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R^{\prime})\circ f\in\mathcal{J}
and thus
\operatorname{id}_{R}=\rho\circ(\mathrm{coev}_{{Q}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R^{\prime})\circ f
, for a retraction
\rho
, also lies in
\mathcal{J}
. Similarly one shows that
\operatorname{id}_{R^{\prime}}
lies in
\mathcal{J}
.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
A
be an exact algebra object. Let
\mathcal{J}
be an ideal in
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
f\in\mathcal{J}(R,R^{\prime})
. For any indecomposable
R,R^{\prime}\in\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, we have

\mathcal{J}(R,R^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(R,R^{\prime})\text{ if }\operatorname{id}_{R}\text{ or }\operatorname{id}_{R^{\prime}}\text{ lies in }\mathcal{J}\\
0\text{ if }\operatorname{id}_{R}\text{ or }\operatorname{id}_{R^{\prime}}\text{ doesn't lie in }\mathcal{J}.\end{cases}

In particular,
\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(R,R^{\prime})=0
if exactly one of
\operatorname{id}_{R^{\prime}},\operatorname{id}_{R}
lies in
\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

The first case is trivial and holds for any ideal. The latter follows immediately from
7.2
.
∎

We now give a module-theoretic proof of a well-known result about exact algebra objects:

Proposition 7.4
.

An exact algebra object is a finite direct product of simple exact algebras.

Proof.

Let
A
be an exact algebra object. Let
J
be an ideal in
A
and
\mathcal{J}
be the corresponding ideal in
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
. Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{R\mid R\in\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\text{ is indecomposable and }\operatorname{id}_{R}\in\mathcal{J}\right\}
, and similarly let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{R\mid R\in\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)\text{ is indecomposable and }\operatorname{id}_{R}\not\in\mathcal{J}\right\}
. Then
\operatorname{add}\mathcal{P}
and
\operatorname{add}\overline{\mathcal{P}}
both define
\mathcal{C}
-module subcategories of
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(R,R^{\prime})=0
if
R\in\mathcal{P},R^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
or vice-versa, by
7.3
.
Let
R,R^{\prime}
be indecomposable objects in
\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, and set
L\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\topp(R),L^{\prime}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\topp(R^{\prime})
. We have

\displaystyle\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R,R^{\prime})\geq\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}R,L^{\prime})=\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(R,{Q}^{\vee}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L^{\prime})=[{Q}^{\vee}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L^{\prime}:L].

Thus, for
R\in\mathcal{P}
and
R^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
,
there is no
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
such that
L^{\prime}
is a subquotient of
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L
and vice-versa. This implies the same statement for
Q\in\mathcal{C}
, and thus partitions
\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A))
into two equivalence classes with respect to the relation of
[
EGNO
, Lemma 7.6.6]
, which yields a splitting of
\mathcal{C}
-module categories
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)={(\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A))}_{1}\oplus{(\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A))}_{2}
by
[
EGNO
, Proposition 7.6.7]
, and in turn also a splitting
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)=\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A_{1})\oplus\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A_{2})
.
This yields a decomposition
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
.
Since
\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A))
is a finite set, the result follows.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

In the setting of the proof of
7.4
we may in fact describe the splitting
A=A_{1}\times A_{2}
explicitly. Let
\mathfrak{I}
be the ideal in
\operatorname{Kl}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}\otimes A}
determined by
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{add}\overline{\mathcal{P}}}(-,-)
, let
\mathtt{I}
be the corresponding mixed subfunctor of
{\mathcal{C}(-,A)}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}
, and
I
the corresponding ideal in
A
. Similarly, let
\mathtt{J}
be the mixed subfunctor corresponding to
J
. There is an isomorphism
\mathtt{I}\simeq{\mathcal{C}(-,A)}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}/\mathtt{J}
, which yields an isomorphism
I\simeq A/J
of
A
-
A
-bimodules, and thus we may set
A_{1}=A/I
and
A_{2}=A/J
as the algebra splitting.

If the base field
\Bbbk
is perfect, then the tensor product of two simple finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-algebras is always semisimple, in other words, the tensor product of two exact algebras in
\mathbf{vecfd}
remains exact. This does not extend to general finite symmetric tensor categories:

Example 7.4
.

Let
\Bbbk
be perfect and
G
an infinitesimal group scheme over
\Bbbk
. Then the coordinate ring
\Bbbk[G]
is a simple (commutative) algebra in
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}G
for the left regular
G
-action, it is in fact
\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Rep}G)
in the notation of
Appendix B
. However, the tensor product
\Bbbk[G]\otimes\Bbbk[G]
is not an exact algebra. For a concrete example, consider the affine group scheme
\mu_{2}
of second roots of unity, which is infinitesimal if and only if
\mathrm{char}(\Bbbk)=2
. Now
\mathcal{C}
is the category of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded vector spaces and we consider the simple algebra
A=\Bbbk[x]/x^{2}
with
x
in odd degree. Then
A\otimes A
is a direct product of simple algebras (
A\times A
) if and only if
\mathrm{char}(\Bbbk)\not=2
.

8.
The radical of a module object and the maximal semisimple quotient

The following definition is analogous to
Subsection 6.1

Definition 8.0
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an abelian monoidal category whose tensor product is right exact in both variables. Let
(B,\mu_{B},\nabla_{B})
be an algebra object in
\mathcal{D}
, let
(M,\nabla_{M})\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{D}}(B)
, and let
I
be an ideal in
B
, with inclusion morphism
\iota_{I}
into
B
. We define the module
MI
by

MI=\operatorname{Im}(M\otimes I\xrightarrow{M\otimes\iota_{J}}M\otimes B\xrightarrow{\nabla_{M}}M).

Analogously to
6.1
, for
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
I
an ideal in
A
, we obtain a description of
{\mathcal{C}(-,MI)}_{|\mathcal{C}_{p}}
by Day convolution in
[\mathcal{C}_{p}^{\operatorname{op}},\mathbf{vecfd}]
:

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and let
I
be an ideal in
A
. Then
{\mathcal{C}({-},MI)}_{|\mathcal{C}_{p}}
is given by

\mathcal{C}(P,MI)=\left\{\nabla_{M}\circ(g\otimes h)\circ\varphi\mid g\in\mathcal{C}(Q,M),h\in\mathcal{C}(Q^{\prime},I)\text{ and }\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q\otimes Q^{\prime})\right\}.

The proof technique for
8.2
is analogous to that for
6.3
.

Proposition 8.2
.

We have

\displaystyle(M\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A))(P)

\displaystyle=\left\{\nabla\circ(g\otimes((q\otimes A)\circ h^{1}))\circ\varphi\ \Bigg|\ \begin{aligned} &g\in\mathcal{C}(Q,M),\,q\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muQ^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{1},\\
&h\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}(Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}),\,\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q\otimes Q^{\prime})\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle=\{\,{(g\circ h)}^{1}\mid g\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{-}A}(Q\otimes A,M),h\in\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}(P,Q)\,\}.

Proof.

The first equality follows immediately by combining
Subsection 5.1
with
8.1
.
The "
\subseteq
" direction of the second equality follows by the left diagram below,
and the "
\supseteq
" direction is a consequence of the right one.

\hskip 2.45836pth\hskip 2.45836pt
\hskip 2.45836ptg\hskip 2.45836pt
=
\hskip 9.01396pth\hskip 9.01396pt
\hskip 2.45836ptg\hskip 2.45836pt
=
\hskip 2.45836pth\hskip 2.45836pt
\hskip 2.45836ptg\hskip 2.45836pt
\hskip 4.09723pt\varphi\hskip 4.09723pt
\hskip 2.45836ptg\hskip 2.45836pt
\hskip 4.09723pth\hskip 4.09723pt
=
\hskip 1.63885pt\varphi\hskip 1.63885pt
\hskip 2.45836ptg\hskip 2.45836pt
\hskip 1.63885pth\hskip 1.63885pt

∎

It is easy to verify the following claims:

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
I
be an ideal in
A
. Restriction
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A/I)\longrightarrow\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
along the algebra morphism
A\twoheadrightarrow A/I
realises
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A/I)
as a full
\mathcal{C}
-module subcategory of
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
which is closed under subquotients. An
A
-module object
M
lies in the image of this functor if and only if
MI=0
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
I,J
be ideals in
A
and let
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
. Then
(MI)J=M(IJ)
.

While
8.5
below is a direct consequence of
8.3
, we present it separately with proof, because of its crucial role in the proof of
Theorem 8.8
.

Lemma 8.5
.

For
Q^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
and
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, if
\,(Q^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M)I\neq 0
, then
MI\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathcal{C}(Q,Q^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M)
,
h\in\mathcal{C}(Q^{\prime},I)
, and
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(P,Q\otimes Q^{\prime})
.
Consider the following diagram,
with a non-zero element
\nabla_{Q^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M}\circ(g\otimes h)\circ\varphi\in((Q^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M)I)(P)

as its left side:

\hskip 4.09723pt\varphi\hskip 4.09723pt
\hskip 2.45836ptg\hskip 2.45836pt
\hskip 4.09723pth\hskip 4.09723pt
\neq 0\implies
M
Q^{\prime\prime}
\hskip 4.09723pt\varphi\hskip 4.09723pt
\hskip 2.45836ptg\hskip 2.45836pt
\hskip 4.09723pth\hskip 4.09723pt
M
\neq 0

The boxes on the right side indicate that the mate of the given non-zero element of
((Q^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M)I)(P)
defines a non-zero element in
(MI)({\vphantom{\!Q^{\prime\prime}}}^{\vee}\!Q^{\prime\prime}\otimes P)
.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category, let
P_{1}^{M}\xrightarrow{p_{1}^{N}}P_{0}^{N}\xrightarrow{p_{0}^{N}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow N\rightarrow 0
be an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
and
M\xrightarrow{g}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow N
an epimorphism in
\mathcal{A}
.

The epimorphism
g
is split if and only if there is a lift
\widehat{g}\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muP_{0}^{N}\rightarrow M
of
g
such that
\widehat{g}\circ p_{1}^{N}=0
.

Proof.

If
g
is split, then a lift
\widehat{g}
as specified can be easily obtained from horseshoe lemma. If there is a left
\widehat{g}
such that
\widehat{g}\circ p_{1}^{N}
, then, since
N
is the cokernel of
p_{1}^{N}
, there is a unique map
h\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muN\rightarrow M
, such that
h\circ p_{0}^{N}=\widehat{g}
. We then find
g\circ h\circ p_{0}^{N}=g\circ\widehat{g}=p_{0}^{N}
, and so
g\circ h=\operatorname{id}_{N}
, since
p_{0}^{N}
is an epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

For
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
non-zero, we have
M\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)\neq M
.

Proof.

Let
k
be such that
{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}^{k}=0
. Thus,
M{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}^{k}=0
. On the other hand, by
8.4
, if
M\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)=M
, then
M{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}^{k}=M
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 8.8
.

The category
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A))
, viewed as a full subcategory of
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, consists of the subquotients of objects of the form
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L
, for
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
and
L
a semisimple object in
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
.

Proof.

First, by
8.7
, for any simple object
L
of
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, we have
L\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)=0
. Thus the semisimple objects in
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
lie in
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A))
, and so do the objects of the form
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L
for
L
semisimple, as well as their subquotients.

It remains to show that any object of
\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A))
is of that form.
Fix an epimorphism
Q\xrightarrow{q}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{1}
with
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
, let
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A))
, and let

0\longrightarrow N\xrightarrow{f}M\xrightarrow{g}L\longrightarrow 0

be a short exact sequence with
L
non-zero semisimple. We now show that the epimorphism
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g
is split.
If
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L
is projective, then the epimorphism
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g
is split.
Otherwise, using
6.8
, the minimal projective presentation
r_{1}^{N}\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muR_{1}^{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L}\rightarrow R_{0}^{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L}
is non-zero and lies in
{\left(\operatorname{Rad}_{A}^{\mathcal{C}}\right)}^{\mathsf{c}}
. Choose splittings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{1}^{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q^{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{0}^{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q^{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}A,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
​​ with
Q_{0},Q_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
.
Using the projectivity of
R_{0}^{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L}
, let
R_{0}^{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L}\xrightarrow{\widehat{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g}}Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M
be a lift of
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g
. Assume that
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g
is not split. Then by
8.6
, we have
\widehat{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g}\circ r_{1}^{N}\neq 0
. By
8.2
, the morphism
{(\widehat{Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g}\circ r_{1}\circ\pi_{1})}^{1}
then defines a non-zero element of
((Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M)\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A))(Q^{1})
. Thus,
((Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M)\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A))\neq 0
and, by
8.5
,
M\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)\neq 0
, which contradicts our assumption. Thus,
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}g
is split.

By induction on a semisimple filtration for
M
, we obtain
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M=\bigoplus_{L\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A))}{(Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}L)}^{\oplus[M:L]}
. Finally,
M
is the image of
Q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M
via the epimorphism
q\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}M
.
∎

Finally, we show that
A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is exact, and hence it can be viewed as the appropriate analogue of the
maximal semisimple quotient

B/\operatorname{Rad}(B)
for a finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-algebra
B
.

Definition 8.8
.

Let
I
be an ideal in
A
, and let
J
be an ideal in
A/I
. We denote by
J_{I}
the lift of
J
to an ideal of
A
, obtained by the indicated pullback in the category of
A
-
A
-bimodules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A/I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J_{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Directly from
Section 8
, and the preservation of monomorphisms under pullbacks, we deduce the following property:

Lemma 8.9
.

For ideals
J,J^{\prime}
in
A/I
, there is an inclusion
J_{I}J^{\prime}_{I}\hookrightarrow{(JJ^{\prime})}_{I}
.

Corollary 8.10
.

If
I
and
J
are nilpotent, then
J_{I}
is nilpotent.

Proof.

Let
n
be such that
J^{n}=0
. Iterating
8.9
, we find a monomorphism
{(J_{I})}^{n}\hookrightarrow{(J^{n})}_{I}=I
. The result follows by observing that, generally, for
I^{\prime}\hookrightarrow I
, we have
JI^{\prime}\hookrightarrow JI
. Alternatively, note that
I\hookrightarrow\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
and so
J_{I}^{n}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, so
J_{I}^{n}
is nilpotent and thus so is
J_{I}
.
∎

Proposition 8.11
.

The algebra
A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
has no nilpotent ideals.

Proof.

Let
J
be a nilpotent ideal in
A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
. Then, by
8.10
,
J_{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}
is nilpotent, and so the composite
J_{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}}A\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is zero. Since epimorphisms in an abelian category are preserved under pullbacks, the morphism
\widetilde{\pi}
in the following pullback square is epic:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A/\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J_{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{J}}

Thus
0=\pi\circ\iota_{\mathtt{Rad}^{\mathcal{C}}(A)}=\iota_{J}\circ\widetilde{\pi}
implies
\iota_{J}=0
, which in turns implies
J=0
.
∎

Appendix A
\mathcal{C}
-projective objects and Skryabin’s theorem

Given a
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
and an object
X\in\mathcal{M}
, we denote the
internal
\operatorname{Hom}
functor
associated to
X
, which by definition is the right adjoint of
{-}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}
, by
\lfloor X,{-}\rfloor
. See
[
EGNO
, Section 7.9]
for details on internal
\operatorname{Hom}
, but note that we use a notation different from
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}({-},{-})
used therein.

Definition A.0
.

We say that an object
X
of a
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
is
\mathcal{C}
-projective
if
P\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}X
is projective in
\mathcal{M}
for any
P\in\mathcal{C}_{p}
. We say that
X
is
\mathcal{C}
-non-projective if it is not
\mathcal{C}
-projective.

The following is implied by
[
EGNO
, Theorem 7.10.1]
, as well as
[
SZ
, Proposition 4.3]
:

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a finite
\mathcal{C}
-module category such that
{-}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}}{-}
is right exact in both variables. Then
X\in\mathcal{M}
is
\mathcal{C}
-projective if and only if
\,\lfloor X,{-}\rfloor
is exact.

Our main result, Theorem
7.1
, is closely related to a result of Skryabin,
[
Sk
, Theorem 3.5]
, which shows in particular that for a finite-dimensional Hopf algebra
H
and an
H
-simple (co)module algebra
A
, every Hopf
A
-module is projective as an
A
-module.

Reformulating this into the language of tensor categories, the result implies that if the finite tensor category
\mathcal{C}
admits a fibre functor
\omega\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{vecfd}
, then for a simple algebra object
A
and
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, the module
\omega(M)\in\operatorname{mod}\omega(A)
is projective. On the other hand, Theorem
7.1
shows in particular that, without the requirement of a fibre functor, every
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is
\mathcal{C}
-projective. The following observation connects the two results:

Lemma A.2
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
admits a fibre functor
\omega\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{vecfd}
. Then
M\in\operatorname{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is
\mathcal{C}
-projective if and only if
\,\omega(M)
is projective in
\operatorname{mod}\omega(A)
.

Proof.

For notational convenience, we write the proof for the category of left
A
-modules in
\mathcal{C}
, which is a right
\mathcal{C}
-module category.

The fibre functor allows us to interpret
\mathcal{C}
as the category of finite dimensional left modules over a finite dimensional Hopf algebra
H
, see
[
EGNO
, Chapter 5]
.
Since
\omega(A)
is a
\Bbbk
-algebra equipped with a left Hopf action of
H
, we can define the smash product (also called crossed product)
A\sharp H
. This is a
\Bbbk
-algebra with
\omega(A)\otimes H
as underlying vector space and multiplication given by

(a\otimes h)(a^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime})\;=\;a(h_{1}a^{\prime})\otimes h_{2}h^{\prime}.

It then follows that the category of left
A
-modules in
\mathcal{C}
is equivalent with the category of finite dimensional left
A\sharp H
-modules.

We can interpret
\omega(A)
as a subalgebra of
A\sharp H
and, for every
A
-module
M
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have an isomorphism

(A\sharp H)\otimes_{\omega(A)}\omega(M)\;\longrightarrow\;M\otimes H,\quad a\otimes h\otimes v\longmapsto a(h_{1}v)\otimes h_{2}

of
A\sharp H
-modules.

Now, by definition
M
is
\mathcal{C}
-projective if and only if the target in this isomorphism is projective. So if
\omega(M)
is projective, it follows that
M
is
\mathcal{C}
-projective.
For the converse, assume that
M
is
\mathcal{C}
-projective. Then
\omega(M\otimes H)
is projective as an
\omega(A)
-module, since
A\sharp H
is projective as an
\omega(A)
-module. But
\omega(M)
is a direct summand of
\omega(M\otimes H)
, so also projective.
∎

Appendix B
Incompressible finite symmetric tensor categories

In this section, we assume that
\Bbbk
is an
algebraically closed
field and that
\mathcal{C}
is a finite
symmetric
tensor category. A finite symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{D}
is
incompressible
if every symmetric tensor functor from
\mathcal{D}
to another symmetric tensor category is fully faithful.

Via Tannakian reconstruction, the structure theory of symmetric tensor categories can be reduced to the classification and study of incompressible symmetric tensor categories, see
[
CEO
]
. All currently known incompressible categories are introduced in
[
BE
,
BEO
,
Co1
]
.

Proposition B.1
.

There exists a (unique) simple commutative algebra
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})
in
\mathcal{C}
that contains all other simple commutative algebras.

Proof.

If
A
and
B
are simple commutative algebras, then they are included in a simple quotient algebra of
A\otimes B
. To conclude the proof we thus only need to show that any chain

A_{1}\subset A_{2}\subset A_{3}\subset\cdots

of simple commutative algebras in
\mathcal{C}
must stabilise. By
Theorem 7.1
, the algebras are exact, which implies that their module categories are tensor categories and we obtain a chain of surjective (every object in the target is a subquotient of one in the essential image) symmetric tensor functors

\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathrm{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}A_{1}\longrightarrow\mathrm{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}A_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}A_{3}\longrightarrow\cdots,

given by extension of scalars. By
[
EGNO
, Proposition 6.3.4]
, the Frobenius–Perron dimension of the categories must decrease along the chain and all functors
\mathrm{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}A_{i}\longrightarrow\mathrm{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}A_{i+1}
must be equivalences, for
i\gg 1
. Since the right adjoints of the functors are given by restriction along
A_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i+1}
it follows that the latter is an isomorphism.
∎

Example B.1
.

(1)

Let
G
be a finite group and
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
, then
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{F}(G)
is the algebra of
\Bbbk
-valued functions on
G
.

(2)

More generally, if
\mathcal{C}
is the category of modules over a cocommutative finite dimensional Hopf algebra
H
, then
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})=H^{\ast}
.

(3)

Let
G
be a finite group with a central element
z
of order
2
. Then for
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(G,z)
as in
[
De
, Examples 0.4]
, we have
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{F}(G/\langle z\rangle)
.

The following improves upon
[
CEO
, Theorem A]
for finite tensor categories.

Proposition B.2
.

There is, up to equivalence, a unique incompressible symmetric tensor category to which
\mathcal{C}
admits a surjective tensor functor. This category can be realised as
\mathrm{mod}_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

It is well-known that surjective symmetric tensor functors out of finite tensor categories correspond precisely to extension of scalars along commutative exact algebras, see
[
CEO
, § 6.3]
. The result thus follows from
B.1
and
Theorem 7.1
.
∎

Example B.2
.

The following conditions are equivalent on a finite symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
:

(1)

\mathcal{C}
is incompressible;

(2)

\mathbb{1}=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})
;

(3)

the only simple commutative algebra in
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathbb{1}
.

We conclude this appendix with a conjectural partial description of
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})
. If the characteristic of
\Bbbk
is zero, then
Appendix B
deals with all cases, by
[
De
, Corollaire 0.7]
. Although non-essential, we thus focus on
\mathrm{char}(\Bbbk)=p>0
.

Let
F\!\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a surjective tensor functor to an incompressible symmetric tensor category. By
B.2
we find, for every
X\in\mathcal{C}

\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{C}(X,\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C}))\;=\;\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{D}(FX,\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{D}}).

A natural property of an incompressible category is that it be
maximally nilpotent
, as defined in
[
Co2
]
. For instance, if
[
Co2
, Conjecture 3.2.3]
is true (as verified for
p=2
in
[
CEO
, Theorem 9.2.1]
) and
[
BEO
, Conjecture 1.4]
is also true, then every incompressible category is maximally nilpotent. Under this assumption on
\mathcal{D}
,
[
CEO
, Theorem 7.2.2 and Remark 7.1.2]
show that

\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{D}(FX,\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{D}})\;=\;\limsup_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\frac{\log(\operatorname{FPdim}\operatorname{Sym}^{\leq n}FX)}{\log(n)},

where
\operatorname{Sym}^{\leq n}
stands for the direct sum of the first
n

symmetric powers of an object. Since Frobenius–Perron dimension of objects is preserved by tensor functors, see
[
EGNO
, Proposition 4.5.7]
, we thus find a conjectural description of
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})
:

Conjecture B.3
.

For every
X\in\mathcal{C}
,

\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{C}(X,\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C}))\;=\;\limsup_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\frac{\log(\operatorname{FPdim}\operatorname{Sym}^{\leq n}X)}{\log(n)}.

Example B.3
.

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
as in
Appendix B
(1), then both sides in
B.3
give
\dim_{\Bbbk}X
. If
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(G,z)
as in
Appendix B
(3), then both sides in
B.3
give the dimension of the even subspace of the super vector space
X
.
